ParlaMint-GR_2016-08-03-S1-commons.u347	Let me give Mr. Spirtzi some time. Ladies and gentlemen, today an important bill is being completed, covering a gap that exists. There were legislation on concessions. However, they were obviously imperfect and, as Mr Spiritzis put it directly, we had the development of this type of projects with many problems, in each category of them and in every period. Let me, however, because they have also been set on ideological terms by Mr Metarakis, both the concession contracts and our understanding of PPPs in general, to clarify a few things. Firstly, the public usually is and once was responsible in Greece to do three things in relation to the country's projects: firstly, to plan projects, secondly, to supervise the implementation of the projects and, thirdly, to finance projects. No accidental or symptoms at all, the first ability the public has to lose. It was lost through successive stages of weakening the institutions and procedures through which it is planned and decided, both on strategic choices in public projects and in the country's infrastructure and on a number of other issues. Its second ability to oversee it was also gradually weakened, as accumulated successive governments continuously weakened the institutions that had this function or competence. The third and most important, obviously in the conditions of economic crisis, fiscal pressure or anything else, but not only for reasons like these, in circumstances where it was felt that cheap private money and private borrowing would be the solution to all the problems of the economy, was also converted into the idea that financing public projects made it private resources, i.e. bank resources. These three dimensions, which I have just suggested and which are obviously the responsibility of successive governments, have led to an even more dramatic development, that is, the idea, in the late 1990s and early 2000s, that all major projects will be carried out in the form of PPPs. For comparative reasons - and I would remind you that the average PPPs in the European Union are 8%. 92% of projects in the European Union are public: infrastructure, airports, ports, what we regard as infrastructure projects. Let me remind you that from 1996, 1998 to the 2008 crisis, the PPPs absorbed 70% of the EDF resources - and I refer to these specific projects, major projects - and the vast majority, obviously, of the funds of the Public Investment Programme. That was the Greek peculiarity. This was one of the causes of the crisis by a general confession. This is the situation that the government is trying to face, which is obviously called upon to close a cycle of major and important projects, by improving, by dismantling these works, which had been frozen. I would remind you that these projects have undergone two revisions of the conventions, that after the second revision they were left out of resources. I would remind you that the main revisions are the public taking on more and more of the risk. In the last review we were in the opposition, we put it directly, as all these predictions, as they had been, were causing problems. So it's not ideological obsession. We are trying to restore - and this is our commitment - firstly, the balance that must exist. The public must be given the opportunity to plan again, it must be possible again to oversee and, of course, to have the opportunity to finance again and to a significant extent. That's what the reality is. At the same time, we do not reject the PPPs, nor concessions, nor any form or tool, if it is considered - and is considered - appropriate or more suitable for one or more categories of projects in order to move forward in this form and not in the form of a public project. I would like to point out here that there is another institutional gap at the level of the government and the way the sector works. Too many European countries - I stress this, too many European countries - have started the process of developing methodological tools, such as public benchmarking. Public benchmarking is a process by which a public body decides whether it is more interesting for a project to be auctioned as a classic public project, whether as a concession or as a public-private partnership. Comparing alternative procedures from an economic point of view - from the point of view of the net present value - and from the point of view of the social interest. Because according to the criterion of whether all the projects were PPPs, as you can see, the only projects that can be done are projects that have a very high future resource flow and can depreciate the project. This is not necessarily a criterion for all projects. It is therefore necessary, firstly, for the public to have a design capacity, secondly, to compare any contracting entity with what is the most appropriate form, from an economic and social point of view, to choose one or other way of building and financing the project. It is no coincidence that this process, widespread - I repeat - in countries such as Portugal, the Netherlands - I stress it - is compulsory - public benchmarking - before starting a project. Before deciding, in other words, how the work is to be done, there must be such a procedure. This is the mark, I think, of our own government. This is the difference in our strategy towards such an important and crucial area. The two laws that we are currently introducing in Parliament are the laws that create a complete, uniform framework for both public procurement and public concessions. Other legislations will follow in the same direction, which have common rules, more transparency, better competition, lower costs, public benefit, and, at the same time, a change of policy on the substance, which will enable the public to play the role that must be played, in order to have infrastructures that the country needs, which have a large social and economic footprint and, at the same time, ensure public interest and social total. Thank you.
